{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.08 -6.01 h 11.66 v 12.01 h -11.66 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.23996pt}{-0.82639pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 42.75 12.18 L 42.75 -11.62}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{30.89293pt}{-8.60004pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\text{$p$ not surjective}\right\}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

which is a finite limit, hence exists, which proves the existence of
i_{*}
.

Next, we assume that
\mathcal{F}
is cofibrant, and we will show that
i_{*}\mathcal{F}
is cofibrant.
That is, given a degeneracy
[k]\rightarrowtriangle[k^{\prime}]
the action
i_{*}\mathcal{F}([k^{\prime}])\rightarrow i_{*}\mathcal{F}([k])
is a complemented inclusion (by
Theorem

4.6
).
The map
i_{*}\mathcal{F}([k])\rightarrow\mathord{\Delta}[n]([k])
gives a decomposition of the map above into a coproduct indexed by all the map
\alpha\colon[k]\to[n]
, so it is enough to show that the fiber above each such map is a complemented inclusion. The fiber over such a map
\alpha
of
i_{*}\mathcal{F}([k])
, is by definition of
i_{*}
the object classifying maps
P\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
over
\mathord{\partial\mathord{\Delta}[n]}
where
P
is the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}[k]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\partial\mathord{\Delta}[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}[n]\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}
\alpha

The fiber of
i_{*}\mathcal{F}([k^{\prime}])
over
\alpha
is described similarly with
P^{\prime}
the pullback of
\mathord{\Delta}[k^{\prime}]\rightarrow\mathord{\Delta}[n]
, and the map we are interested in is induced by the map
P^{\prime}\rightarrow P
obtained as the pullback of
\mathord{\Delta}[k^{\prime}]\rightarrow\mathord{\Delta}[k]
. But it follows from
[
24
]
*Proposition 3.1.11 that a pullback of a degeneracy operator is an iterated pushout of degeneracy operators, in this case a finite such iterated pushout as
P^{\prime}
is finite.
As
\mathcal{F}
is cofibrant, this decomposes
\mathcal{F}(P)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(P^{\prime})
as a composite of complemented inclusions, and hence concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

6.5
.

We show that all cofibrations with cofibrant domain are in
\mathcal{G}
.
By
Lemma

4.4
, it suffices to show that the generating cofibrations are in
\mathcal{G}
and that
\mathcal{G}
is closed under operations appearing in a cell complex.
The case of generators is
Proposition

6.10
.
Closure under tensoring by objects of
\mathcal{E}
is
Proposition

6.9
, closure under pushout (along maps with cofibrant target) is
Proposition

6.7
, and closure under sequential composition is
Proposition

6.8
.
∎

An analysis of the proof of
Theorem

6.5
shows that the assumption that
A
is cofibrant is not needed for the exponentiability of
i
, as it is only used for the part of the argument regarding preservation of cofibrant objects by
i_{*}
.

7
The Frobenius property

We adapt the notion of a strong homotopy equivalence and the associated concepts from
[
20
]
*Section 3 to our setting.
Recall that a map
f\colon A\to B
is a 0-oriented (respectively,
1-oriented
) homotopy equivalence if there is a map
g\colon B\to A
with homotopies
u\colon gf\sim\operatorname{id}_{A}
and
v\colon fg\sim\operatorname{id}_{B}
(respectively,
u\colon\operatorname{id}_{A}\sim gf
and
v\colon id_{B}\sim fg
).
Such a homotopy equivalence is called
strong
if the homotopies satisfy the coherence condition
fu=vf
.

We recall the abstract characterisation of strong homotopy equivalences.
The commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathord{\varnothing}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}[1]}
!
!
\lambda^{0}_{1}
\lambda^{1}_{1}

induces maps
\theta_{0}\colon!\to\lambda^{0}_{1}
and
\theta_{1}\colon!\to\lambda^{1}_{1}
in the arrow category of
\mathsf{sSet}
.
(We will use
\lambda^{i}_{k}
to denote the horn inclusion
\mathord{\Lambda}^{i}[k]\to\mathord{\Delta}[k]
.)
Note that
!
is the unit of the pushout tensor and pullback cotensor of the enrichment of
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
in
\mathsf{sSet}
.
Recall that pushout tensors with levelwise complemented inclusions between finite simplicial sets such as
!,\lambda^{0}_{1},\lambda^{0}_{1}
exist by
Proposition

5.5
.

7.1
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
.
For
k\in\{0,1\}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is a
k
-oriented strong homotopy equivalence,

(ii)

\theta_{k}\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\cdot}}}f\colon f\to\lambda^{k}_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\cdot}}}f
is a split monomorphism,

(iii)

\theta_{k}\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\pitchfork}}}f\colon\lambda^{k}_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\pitchfork}}}f\to f
is a split epimorphism.

Proof.

Identical to
[
20
]
*Lemma 4.3 and
[
19
]
*Lemma 3.1.1.
∎

7.2
.

Let
i
be a levelwise complemented inclusion between finite simplicial sets that is a strong homotopy equivalence.
For any map
f
in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
, the pushout tensor
i\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\cdot}}}f
is a strong homotopy equivalence in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

This is a formal consequence of the characterisation
(ii)
of strong homotopy equivalences given by
Lemma

7.1
.
We have
\theta_{k}\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\cdot}}}(i\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\cdot}}}f)\mathrel{\cong}(\theta_{k}\mathbin{\widehat{\times}}i)\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\cdot}}}f
, a formal consequence of the isomorphism
A\mathbin{\cdot}(B\mathbin{\cdot}X)\mathrel{\cong}(A\times B)\mathbin{\cdot}X
natural in
A,B\in\mathsf{sSet}
and
X\in\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
.
By assumption,
\theta_{k}\mathbin{\widehat{\times}}i
has a retraction, hence also its image under
(-)\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\cdot}}}f
.
∎

Strong homotopy equivalences can be used to relate cofibrations and trivial cofibrations.

7.3
.

(i)

For a horn inclusion
j\in J_{\mathsf{sSet}}
and
E\in\mathcal{E}
, the map
j\mathbin{\cdot}E
is a strong homotopy equivalence and cofibration between cofibrant objects.

(ii)

Any cofibration that is a strong homotopy equivalence is a trivial cofibration.

Proof.

For
(i)
, recall from
[
18
]
*Chapter IV, Section 2, Paragraph 2.1.3 that the horn inclusion
j
in
\mathsf{sSet}
is a strong homotopy equivalence.
By
Corollary

7.2
, it follows that
j\mathbin{\cdot}E
is a strong homotopy equivalence.
The object
E\in\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is cofibrant by
(i)
of
Proposition

5.9
.
By
Proposition

5.5
, it follows that
j\mathbin{\cdot}E
is a cofibration between cofibrant objects.

(ii)
follows from the characterisation of strong homotopy equivalences in
(ii)
of
Lemma

7.1
, closure of trivial cofibrations under retracts (
Lemma

3.9
), and
Proposition

5.5
(using that
\lambda^{0}_{1}
and
\lambda^{1}_{1}
are trivial cofibrations).
∎

7.4
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
g
f

be a pullback square with
X
cofibrant. If,
f
is a
k
-oriented strong homotopy equivalence, where
k\in\{0,1\}
, then so
is
g
.

Proof.

This is identical to
[
19
]
*Lemma 3.1.3, but played out in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
instead of
\mathsf{sSet}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.
The pushout product with
\{1\}\to\mathord{\Delta}[1]
(for
k=0
) becomes a pushout tensor, which sends the cofibration
\varnothing\to X
to a trivial cofibration by
Proposition

5.5
.
∎

7.5
.

Let
f\colon X\twoheadrightarrow Y
be a Kan fibration with
X
cofibrant.
The pullback functor
f^{*}\colon\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\downarrow}Y\to\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\downarrow}X
preserves maps that in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
are strong homotopy equivalences with cofibrant target.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

7.4
using
(ii)
of
Lemma

1.5
and stability of cofibrant objects under pullback along maps with cofibrant source using
(ii)
of
Proposition

5.9
.
∎

7.6
Frobenius property.

Let
f\colon X\twoheadrightarrow Y
be a Kan fibration with
X
cofibrant.
The pullback functor
f^{*}\colon\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\downarrow}Y\to\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\downarrow}X
preserves trivial cofibrations.

Proof.

Let
j
be a trivial cofibration over
Y
.
By
Proposition

3.17
, its underlying map in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
can be written as a retract of a
J_{\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}}
-cell complex
j^{\prime}
.
The retraction (including
j^{\prime}
) lifts uniquely to the slice over
Y
.
Since functors preserve retracts, this makes
f^{*}j
a retract of
f^{*}j^{\prime}
.
By
Lemma

3.9
, it will thus suffice to show that
f^{*}j^{\prime}
is a trivial cofibration.

Recall that
J_{\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}}
consists of levelwise complemented inclusions.
By countable lextensivity,
Lemma

3.8
, and
Corollary

2.12
, the pullback functor
f^{*}
preserves the colimits (countable coproducts, pushouts, sequential colimit) forming the cell complex
j^{\prime}
.
By
Lemma

3.9
, it thus remains to show that
f^{*}
sends to a trivial cofibration any map that in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is of the form
E\times\underline{j^{\prime\prime}}
where
E\in\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
and
j^{\prime\prime}\in J_{\mathsf{sSet}}
.
Using
Lemma

5.4
, this simplifies to
j^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\cdot}E
.
Here, we see
E
as a constant simplicial object in
\mathcal{E}
.

By
(i)
of
Corollary

7.3
,
j^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\cdot}E
is a strong homotopy equivalence and cofibration between cofibrant objects.
By
Corollary

7.5
,
f^{*}(j^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\cdot}E)
is a strong homotopy equivalence (using that
f
is a Kan fibration).
By
(i)
,
f^{*}(j^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\cdot}E)
is a cofibration between cofibrant objects.
By
(ii)
of
Corollary

7.3
, we conclude that
f^{*}(j^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\cdot}E)
is a trivial cofibration.
∎

8
Fibration extension properties

In this section, we establish two important ingredients in the construction of the effective model structure:
the trivial fibration extension property (
Proposition

8.5
) and the fibration extension property (
Proposition

8.13
).
These arguments are based on the equivalence extension property (
Proposition

8.3
).
We work purely within the cofibrant fragment
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
of
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
.
Our earlier preliminaries allow us to prove the equivalence extension property in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
following
[
45
]
*Proposition 5.1 and
[
19
]
*Proposition 3.2.1.

We begin with some observations on homotopy equivalences, which we introduced in
Section

1
,
and an analysis of the restriction of the fibration category structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
established in
Theorem

1.9
to cofibrant objects.
Since the tensor of
X\in\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
with a finite simplicial set exists and is defined by the formula in (
2.1
),
we may equivalently write a homotopy
H
between
f_{0},f_{1}\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
or one of its slices,
which was defined using cotensors in (
1.3
), via a map

H\colon\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}X\to Y\text{.}

(8.1)

In
\mathcal{E}
and its slices, the homotopy relation between maps with cofibrant source and fibrant target is an equivalence relation.
This is a formal consequence of
(i)
of
Lemma

1.5
and
Lemma

1.8
.
It follows that homotopy equivalences between cofibrant and fibrant objects compose as usual.

8.1
.

(i)

For every
X\in\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
, trivial cofibrations in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
are homotopy equivalences.

(ii)

Trivial fibrations
X\to Y
in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
are homotopy equivalences over
Y
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, in
\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
, given a trivial cofibration
A\to B
, we take a lift

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{B}(\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\pitchfork}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}

Here, the right map is a composition of the pullback cotensor with
\partial\mathord{\Delta}[1]\to\mathord{\Delta}[1]
of
B\to 1
and a pullback of
A\to 1
, hence a fibration by parts
(i)
and
(ii)
of
Lemma

1.5
.
The lift exhibits
A\to B
as a strong deformation retract, in particular a homotopy equivalence.

For
(ii)
, given a fibration
X\to Y
in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
, we take a lift

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\sqcup_{X}(\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}

Here, the left map is a composition of a pushout of
\varnothing\to Y
and the pushout tensor with
\partial\mathord{\Delta}[1]\to\mathord{\Delta}[1]
of
\varnothing\to X
, hence a cofibration by
Lemmas

3.9
and
5.5
.
The lift exhibits
X\to Y
as the dual of a strong deformation retract, in particular a homotopy equivalence over
Y
.
∎

8.2
.

Let
X\in\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.
The fibration category structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
of
Theorem

1.9
restricts to
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
.
Path objects are given by cotensor with
\mathord{\Delta}[1]
.
The weak equivalences coincide with homotopy equivalences over
X
.

Proof.

By
(iii)
of
Proposition

5.9
,
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
has finite limits and they are computed as in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\downarrow}X
.
By
(iii)
of
Lemma

5.6
, cotensor with
\mathord{\Delta}[1]
over
X
preserves cofibrant objects.
Thus, all aspects of the fibration category
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
of
Theorem

1.9
restrict to cofibrant objects.
This includes path objects, which are given by cotensor with
\mathord{\Delta}[1]
.

It remains to show that pointwise weak equivalences in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
coincide with homotopy equivalences over
X
.
Every homotopy equivalence is a pointwise weak equivalence by
Proposition

1.10
.
For the reverse direction, we use the mapping path space factorisation in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
, which has a homotopy equivalence over
X
as first factor and fibration as second factor.
Since pointwise weak equivalences and homotopy equivalences over
X
satisfy the 2-out-of-3 property, it suffices to show that every pointwise weak equivalence that is a fibration (hence a trivial fibration) is a homotopy equivalence over
X
.
This is
(ii)
of
Proposition

8.1
.
∎

8.3
Equivalence extension property.

In
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
, consider the solid part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\sim
\sim
i

(8.2)

where the lower square is a pullback and
X_{0}\to X_{1}
is a homotopy equivalence over
A
.
Then there is
Y_{0}
as indicated such that the back square is a pullback and
Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
is a homotopy equivalence over
B
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
19
]
*Proposition 3.2.1 applies, but played out in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
instead of
\mathsf{sSet}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.
We limit ourselves to listing the key claims used in the proof and why they hold in our setting.

•

The slice categories
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}A
and
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}B
admit
fibration category structures, established in
Proposition

8.2
, in which
weak equivalences are given by fiberwise homotopy equivalences.

•

The dependent product functor
i_{*}
along
i
exists and preserves cofibrant objects, as shown in
Theorem

6.5
.

•

The functor
i_{*}
preserves trivial fibrations, which follows by adjointness since
i^{*}
preserves cofibrations, as stated in
(i)
of
Proposition

5.9
.

•

In the slice over
B
, pullback cotensor with a cofibration preserves trivial fibrations, which holds by
Lemma

1.8
.
∎

In
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
, we say that a (trivial) fibration
X\twoheadrightarrow A

extends
along a map
A\to B
if there is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

(8.3)

with the
extension

Y\to B
of
X\to A
again a (trivial) fibration.
If
A\to B
has this property for all (trivial) fibrations
X\twoheadrightarrow A
, we say that it has the
(trivial) fibration extension property
.

8.4
.

Let
f
and
g
be composable maps in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.
If
g\circ f
has the (trivial) fibration extension property, then so does
f
.

Proof.

We extend along
f
by extending along
g\circ f
and pulling back along
g
(using
(ii)
of
Lemma

1.5
and
(ii)
of
Proposition

5.9
).
∎

8.5
Trivial fibration extension property.

Cofibrations in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
have the trivial fibration extension property.

Proof.

This is the special case of
Proposition

8.3
where
X_{1}\to A
and
Y_{1}\to B
are the identities on
A
and
B
, respectively.
We use
Theorems

1.9
and
4.1
to go between trivial fibrations and fibrations that are weak equivalences.
∎

8.6
.

Let
p\colon X\twoheadrightarrow\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A
be fibration in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
with
A
and
X
cofibrant.
Then there is a homotopy equivalence between
X|_{\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}
and
X|_{\{1\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}
over
A
.

Proof.

Take the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\pitchfork}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\pitchfork}(\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A)\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}
\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\pitchfork}p

Here, the bottom map is the unit of the tensor-cotensor adjunction.
The right map is a fibration by
(i)
of
Lemma

1.5
, hence the left map is a fibration by
(ii)
of
Lemma

1.5
.
The top right object is cofibrant is cofibrant by
(i)
of
Lemma

1.5
and
(ii)
, hence the top left object is cofibrant by
(ii)
.

We will argue that there are trivial fibrations from
P
to
X|_{\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}
and
X|_{\{1\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}
over
A
.
These trivial fibrations are homotopy equivalences over
A
by
(ii)
of
Proposition

8.1
.
Inverting and composing them as needed gives the desired weak equivalence.

We only construct the trivial fibration from
P
to
X|_{\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}
(the other case is dual).
Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\pitchfork}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X|_{\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A}\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\pitchfork}(\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\pitchfork}(\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}
\lambda^{0}_{1}\mathbin{\pitchfork}(\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A)
\lambda^{0}_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\pitchfork}}}p
p

The two composite squares and the bottom right square are pullbacks by construction.
Pullback pasting induces the top left map and makes the top left square a pullback.
The top middle map is a trivial fibration by
(i)
of
Lemma

1.5
, hence so is the top left map by
(ii)
of
Lemma

1.5
.
∎

Our aim now is to prove the fibration extension property for trivial cofibrations in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.
For this purpose, we introduce the class
\mathcal{H}
of cofibrations in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
that have the fibration extension property.

8.7
.

The class
\mathcal{H}
contains cofibrations in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
that are strong homotopy equivalences.

Proof.

Let
A\to B
be a cofibration in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
and
0
-oriented strong homotopy equivalence (the
1
-oriented case is dual).
We will solve the extension problem (
8.3
).
By the characterisation of strong homotopy equivalences given by part (3) of
Lemma

7.1
, we have a retract diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A)\sqcup_{\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}(\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}
\lambda^{1}_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}B

(8.4)

Let
Z\to\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A\sqcup_{\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}B
denote the pullback of
X\to A
along the top right map.
Pulling back
Z
to
\mathord{\Delta}[1]\mathbin{\cdot}A
,
\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}A
and
\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}B
(the components of its base pushout),
we obtain the solid part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z|_{\{1\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z|_{\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z|_{\{0\}\mathbin{\cdot}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\hbox to0.0pt{\text{,}\hss}}
\sim
\sim

with lower square a pullback.
Here, the weak equivalences over
A
is given by
Lemma

8.6
.
We then complete the diagram using
Proposition

8.3
, making the back square a pullback.
Note that
Z|_{\{1\}\mathbin{\cdot}A}
is isomorphic to
X
over
A
by the retract (
8.4
).
The extension in (
8.3
) is then given by
Y\twoheadrightarrow B
.
∎

8.8
.

For a horn inclusion
j\in J_{\mathsf{sSet}}
and
E\in\mathcal{E}
, we have
j\mathbin{\cdot}E\in\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

This is the application of
Lemma

8.7
to
(i)
of
Corollary

7.3
.
∎

8.9
.

The class
\mathcal{H}
is closed under countable coproducts.

Proof.

Let
A_{i}\to B_{i}
be a family of maps in
\mathcal{H}
for
i\in I
countable.
Note that
\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i}\to\coprod_{i\in I}B_{i}
is a cofibration between cofibrant objects by
Lemma

3.9
.
Suppose we are given a fibration
X\to\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i}
in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.
We aim to extend it along
\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i}\to\coprod_{i\in I}B_{i}
.
Note that
\coprod_{i\in I}B_{i}
is a van Kampen colimit since
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is countably lextensive.

For each
i\in I
, we pull it back to a fibration
X_{i}\to A_{i}
(with
X_{i}
cofibrant by
(ii)
) and extend it to a fibration
Y_{i}\to B_{i}
.
We take their coproduct
\coprod_{i\in I}Y_{i}\to\coprod_{i\in I}B_{i}
.
This is a fibration by
(i)
of
Lemma

3.18
.
Its domain is cofibrant by
Lemma

3.9
.
By effectivity, it pulls back along
A_{i}\to\coprod_{i\in I}B_{i}
to the map
X_{i}\to A_{i}
for
i\in I
.
By universality, it thus pulls back along
\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i}\to\coprod_{i\in I}B_{i}
to the original fibration
X\to\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i}
.
∎

8.10
.

The class
\mathcal{H}
is closed under pushouts in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
along maps with cofibrant target.

Proof.

Consider a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}
\in\mathcal{H}

with
A^{\prime}
cofibrant.
Note that
A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
is a cofibration between cofibrant objects by
Lemma

3.9
.
The pushout is van Kampen by part (i) of
Corollary

2.12
.
Suppose we are given a fibration
X^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.
We aim to extend it along
A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
.

We pull the given fibration back along
A\to A^{\prime}
to a fibration
X\twoheadrightarrow A
(here,
X
is cofibrant by
(ii)
) and extend it to a fibration
Y\twoheadrightarrow B
.
Let
Y^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
be the pushout in the arrow category of these three maps.
By effectivity, it pulls back to them.
It is a fibration by
(ii)
of
Lemma

3.18
.
By
(i)
,
X\to Y
is a cofibration, hence so is
X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
by
Lemma

3.9
.
This makes
Y^{\prime}
cofibrant.

We check that
Y^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
is a fibration using
Proposition

3.4
.
For each horn inclusion
j\in J_{\mathsf{sSet}}
, we construct a section of
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{j}(Y^{\prime}\to B^{\prime})
given sections of
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{j}(X^{\prime}\to A^{\prime})
and
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{j}(Y\to B)
.
We pull the section of
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{j}(X^{\prime}\to A^{\prime})
back to a section of
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{j}(X\to A)
and then extend it using
Lemma

3.13
to a section of
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{j}(Y\to B)
.
The goal follows by
Lemma

2.15
and functoriality of colimits.
∎

8.11
.

The class
\mathcal{H}
is closed under sequential colimits.

Proof.

Consider the colimit
B
of a sequential diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}
\in\mathcal{H}
\in\mathcal{H}

Note that it is van Kampen by part (ii) of
Corollary

2.12
.
Suppose we are given a fibration
X_{0}\twoheadrightarrow A_{0}
in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.
We aim to extend it along
A_{0}\to B
.

By induction on
k
, we extend to a fibration
X_{k}\twoheadrightarrow A_{k}
.
The maps
X_{k}\to X_{k+1}
are cofibrations by
(i)
.
In the end, we take the colimit and obtain a map
Y\to B
.
By effectivity, it pulls back to the maps
X_{k}\twoheadrightarrow A_{k}
.
It is a fibration by
(iii)
of
Lemma

3.18
.
Note that
Y
is cofibrant by
Lemma

3.9
.
∎

8.12
.

The class
\mathcal{H}
is closed under codomain retracts.

Proof.

This is an instance of
Lemma

8.4
.
∎

8.13
Fibration extension property.

Trivial cofibrations in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
have the fibration extension property.

Proof.

We have to show that
\mathcal{H}
includes all trivial cofibrations between cofibrant objects.
By
Proposition

3.17
, any such trivial cofibration can be written as a codomain retract of a sequential colimit of pushouts of countable coproducts of tensors with objects of
E
of maps in
J_{\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}}
.
By induction, all the stages of the sequential colimit are cofibrant.
This means that the above pushout squares all consist of cofibrant objects.
The claim now follows starting from
Corollary

8.8
using the closure properties of
\mathcal{H}
given by
Lemmas

8.9
,
8.10
,
8.11
and
8.12
.
∎

9
The effective model structure

The main goal of this section is to establish the existence of the effective model structure. Since the categories with which we work have finite limits but do not
necessarily have finite colimits, it is appropriate to consider a slight generalisation of the usual notion of a model structure. For a category
\mathcal{E}
with an initial object and a terminal object, a
model structure
on
\mathcal{E}
consists of three classes of maps
\mathbf{W}
,
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{F}
such that

•

(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{W})
and
(\mathbf{C}\cap\mathbf{W},\mathbf{F})
are weak factorisation systems;

•

\mathbf{W}
satisfies the 2-out-of-3 property;

•

\mathcal{E}
has pushouts along maps in
\mathbf{C}
;

•

\mathcal{E}
has pullbacks along maps in
\mathbf{F}
.

It can then be shown that
\mathbf{W}
is closed under retracts, as the known proof of this fact (see
[
32
]
*Proposition 7.8 and
[
44
]
*Lemma 11.3.3) applies also assuming only the restricted limits and colimits above. Thus, when
\mathcal{E}
is finitely complete and cocomplete, this notion is equivalent to the usual one. Similarly, a model structure is determined by two of its three classes of maps also in this setting.

Let us now fix a countably lextensive category
\mathcal{E}
.
The existence of the effective model structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
will be a formal consequence of the Frobenius property of
Section

7
, the (trivial) fibration extension property of
Section

8
, and elementary properties of the two weak factorisation systems of
Theorem

4.2
.
To this end, we encapsulate what is used from
Section

8
as a collection of extension operations that all follow the same pattern.

9.1
.

The following hold in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.

(i)

Let
A\to B
be a cofibration and
X\to A
be a trivial fibration.
There is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}

with
X\to Y
a cofibration and
Y\to B
a trivial fibration.

(ii)

Let
A\to B
be a trivial cofibration and
X\to A
be a fibration.
There is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}

with
X\to Y
a trivial cofibration and
Y\to B
a fibration.

(iii)

Let
A\to B
be a trivial cofibration and
X\to A
be a trivial fibration.
There is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\hbox to0.0pt{\text{.}\hss}}

with
X\to Y
a trivial cofibration and
Y\to B
a trivial fibration.

Proof.

(i)
is the combination of
Proposition

8.5
with
(i)
of
Proposition

5.9
.

(ii)
is the combination of
Proposition

8.13
with
Proposition

7.6
.

(iii)
follows from
(i)
using
Proposition

7.6
(with
Proposition

5.2
).
∎

Recall from
Section

1
that a map
X\to Y
in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{fib}}
is a weak equivalence in the fibration category of
Theorem

1.7
if and only if it is a
pointwise weak equivalence in the sense of
Definition

1.6
, i.e.,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{sSet}}(E,X)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{sSet}}(E,Y)
is a weak homotopy equivalence of simplicial sets for all
E\in\mathcal{E}
.
Restricting to cofibrant objects, we obtain a notion of weak equivalence in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof},\mathsf{fib}}
that satisfies 2-out-of-3 and interacts as expected with cofibrations and fibrations, as recollected below.

9.2
.

In
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof},\mathsf{fib}}
, we have:

(i)

a cofibration is a trivial cofibration exactly if it is a weak equivalence,

(ii)

a fibration is a trivial fibration exactly if it is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

(ii)
is a corollary of
Proposition

4.1
.
For
(i)
, the forward direction is the combination of
(i)
of
Proposition

8.1
and
Proposition

1.10
.
With this, the reverse direction follows by the retract argument.
∎

In the following, we fix the following terminology regarding the weak factorisation systems of
Theorem

4.2
.
A
fibrant replacement
of
X\in\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is a trivial cofibration
X\to X^{\prime}
with
X^{\prime}
fibrant.
By a fibrant replacement of a diagram, we mean a levelwise fibrant replacement: given a diagram
X\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
, this is a diagram
X^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{fib}}
with a natural transformation
X\to X^{\prime}
that is levelwise a trivial cofibration.
If
\mathcal{S}
is a finite Reedy category, we can always construct such a replacement using
Theorem

3.14
and the Reedy process.
In particular, for
[1]
seen as a direct category, we obtain a fibrant replacement of any arrow that we call
canonical
.
Note that the canonical fibrant replacement preserves trivial cofibrations.
We use dual terminology for
cofibrant replacement
.

Let us write
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
for the class of maps in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
whose canonical fibrant replacement is a weak equivalence in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof},\mathsf{fib}}
.
This will be the class of weak equivalences in the model structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
to be established in
Proposition

9.6
.

9.3
.

Let
A\to B
in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
. Then, the
the following are equivalent:

(i)

the map
A\to B
is in
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
,

(ii)

the map
A\to B
has a fibrant replacement that is a weak equivalence in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof},\mathsf{fib}}
,

(iii)

all fibrant replacements of the map
A\to B
are weak equivalences in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof},\mathsf{fib}}
.

Proof.

This is a standard argument and goes exactly as in
[
19
]
*Lemma 3.3.1.
What is used is part (i) of
Corollary

2.12
with the fact that trivial cofibrations are levelwise complemented inclusions (
Proposition

3.17
), and closure properties of trivial cofibrations (
Lemma

3.9
), the forward direction of
(i)
of
Lemma

9.2
, and 2-out-of-3 for weak equivalences in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof},\mathsf{fib}}
.
∎

9.4
.

The class
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
satisfies the 2-out-of-3 property.

Proof.

Using
Lemma

9.3
with levelwise fibrant replacement of the given 2-out-of-3 diagram, this reduces to closure of weak equivalences in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof},\mathsf{fib}}
under 2-out-of-3.
This is part of
Theorem

1.7
.
∎

9.5
.

In
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
, a fibration is a trivial fibration if and only if it is in
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.

Proof.

Let
X\to Y
be a fibration in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.
Take a fibrant replacement
Y\to\overline{Y}
.

If
X\to Y
is a trivial fibration, we extend it to a trivial fibration
\overline{X}\to\overline{Y}
using
(iii)
of
Lemma

9.1
.
Then
\overline{X}\to\overline{Y}
is a weak equivalence by
(ii)
of
Lemma

9.2
, hence
X\to Y
is in
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
by
Lemma

9.3
.

In the reverse direction, we extend
X\to Y
to a fibration
\overline{X}\to\overline{Y}
using
(ii)
of
Lemma

9.1
.
If
X\to Y
is in
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
, then
\overline{X}\to\overline{Y}
is a weak equivalence by
Lemma

9.3
, hence a trivial fibration by
(ii)
of
Lemma

9.2
.
Then its pullback
X\to Y
is a trivial fibration by
(ii)
of
Lemma

1.5
.
∎

9.6
.

The category
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
admits a model structure with weak equivalences
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
and the two weak factorisation systems of
Theorem

4.2
.

Proof.

First note that
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
has finite limits by
(iii)
of
Proposition

5.9
, an initial object by lextensivity, and pushouts of cofibrations by
(i)
of
Corollary

2.12
(since cofibrations are levelwise complemented inclusions by
Proposition

3.17
).
The class
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
satisfies 2-out-of-3 by
Corollary

9.4
.

It remains to show that a (co)fibration is trivial exactly if it is a weak equivalence.
For fibrations, this is
Lemma

9.5
.
For cofibrations, the forward direction is immediate using
Lemma

9.3
:
a given trivial cofibration has as fibrant replacement the identity on a fibrant replacement of its codomain; but identities are weak equivalences in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof},\mathsf{fib}}
by
Theorem

1.7
.
The backward direction follows from this by the retract argument.
∎

We write
\mathbf{W}
for the class of maps in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
whose canonical cofibrant replacement is in
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
. This is the
class of weak equivalences of the effective model structure, to be established in
Theorem

9.9
.

9.7
.

Let
A\to B
in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
. Then,
the following are equivalent:

(i)

the map
A\to B
is in
\mathbf{W}
,

(ii)

the map
A\to B
has a cofibrant replacement in
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
,

(iii)

all cofibrant replacements of the map
A\to B
are in
\mathbf{W}_{\mathsf{cof}}
.

Proof.

This is a standard argument, dual to the one of
Lemma

9.3
.
What is used is closure properties of trivial fibrations (
(ii)
of
Lemma

1.5
) and the model structure on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
of
Proposition

9.6
.
∎

9.8
.

The class
\mathbf{W}
satisfies the 2-out-of-3 property.

Proof.

This is analogous to the proof of
Corollary

9.4
.
∎

We can finally establish the existence of the effective model structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
.

9.9
The effective model structure.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a countably lextensive category.

(i)

The category
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
of simplicial objects in
\mathcal{E}
admits a model structure determined by the two weak factorisation systems of
Theorem

4.2
.

(ii)

A map between fibrant objects is a weak equivalence in this model structure if and only if it is a pointwise weak equivalence in the sense of
Definition

1.6
.

(iii)

More generally, for
X\in\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
, a map in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\twoheaddownarrow}X
is a weak equivalence exactly if and only if it is a pointwise weak equivalence in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
in the sense of
Definition

1.6
.

Proof.

First note that
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
has finite limits by lextensivity and the required colimits of a model structure by the same reasoning used for
Proposition

9.6
.
We define the class of weak equivalences to be
\mathbf{W}
.
It satisfies 2-out-of-3 by
Corollary

9.4
.
It remains to show that a (co)fibration is trivial exactly if it is a weak equivalence.

Due do our definition of
\mathbf{W}
, we get for free that every trivial fibration is a weak equivalence, dually to the reasoning for trivial cofibrations in
Proposition

9.6
.

For the reverse direction, let
X\to Y
be a fibration and weak equivalence.
Let
\widehat{X}\to\widehat{Y}
denote its canonical cofibrant replacement.
This is the Reedy cofibrant replacement over the inverse category
[1]
, hence again a fibration.
Since
\widehat{X}\to\widehat{Y}
is a fibration and weak equivalence in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
, it is a trivial fibration by
Proposition

9.6
.
The composite
\widehat{X}\to Y
is a trivial fibration by
(ii)
of
Lemma

1.5
.
By
(iii)
of
Lemma

1.5
, we deduce that
X\to Y
is a trivial fibration.

Let
A\to B
be a trivial cofibration.
Take a cofibrant replacement
\widehat{B}\to B
.
Let
\widehat{A}\to A
be its pullback along
A\to B
.
Then
\widehat{A}
is cofibrant by
Lemma

5.7
since trivial cofibrations are
monomorphisms by
Proposition

5.2
,
\widehat{A}\to A
is a trivial fibration by
(ii)
of
Lemma

1.5
, and
\widehat{A}\to\widehat{B}
is a trivial cofibration by
Proposition

7.6
.
In particular,
\widehat{A}\to\widehat{B}
is a cofibrant replacement of
A\to B
.
Since it is a trivial cofibration, it is a weak equivalence in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{cof}}
by
Proposition

9.6
.
By
Lemma

9.7
, this makes
A\to B
is a weak equivalence.

It remains to show that every cofibration that is a weak equivalence is a trivial cofibration.
As in
Proposition

9.6
, this follows from what we have already established by the retract argument.

This finishes the verification of
(i)
.

(ii)
and
(iii)
follow since every model structure induces a fibration category structure on its fibrant objects (and those of its slices) and the weak equivalences in a fibration category are determined by its fibrations and trivial fibrations.
In our case, we obtain the fibration categories of
Theorems

1.7
and
1.9
.
∎

By
(ii)
of
Theorem

9.9
, a map is a weak equivalence in the effective model structure if and only if its fibrant replacement is a pointwise weak equivalence.
This gives us a description of weak equivalences independent from the class
\mathbf{W}
used in the construction of the model structure.

The next remark compares the effective model structure
Theorem

9.9
to other model structures on categories of simplicial objects.

9.10
.

When
\mathcal{E}
, and hence
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
, is a locally presentable, then
one can use the enriched small object argument of
[
44
]
*Chapter 13 to produce the two weak factorisation systems on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
whose fibrations and trivial fibrations are as in
Definition

1.3
.
Theorem

1.7
then implies that
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is a weak model category, for example using the dual of
[
23
]
*Proposition 2.3.3. It then follows from
[
25
]
*Theorem 3.7 that its left saturation (in the sense of
[
25
]
*Theorem 4.1) is a left semi-model category, and from
[
25
]
*Theorem 3.8 that it is also is a right semi-model category. In general, this is not quite enough to conclude that it is a Quillen model category (it is what is called a two-sided model category in
[
25
]
*Section 5), but this is already sufficient for many applications.

When
\mathcal{E}
is an additive locally presentable category, then there is Quillen model structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
whose fibrations and trivial fibrations are exactly as in
Definition

1.3
. The additional ingredient in this case is that for
A\in\mathcal{E}
and
X\in\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
, the object
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{sSet}}(A,X)
is a simplicial abelian group, hence is always a Kan complex. This shows that when
\mathcal{E}
is additive, all objects of
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
are Kan complexes, hence in the discussion above it is immediate that
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is left saturated (in the sense of
[
25
]
) and as it is a saturated right semi-model category where every object is fibrant it is a Quillen model category.
By the Dold–Kan correspondence, the category
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is equivalent to the category of chain complexes
concentrated in non-negative degrees in
\mathcal{E}
and under this equivalence the model structure is the so-called absolute (or Hurewicz) model structure on chain complexes (see, e.g.,
[
9
]
*Corollary 6.4).

A different model structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is established by Quillen in
[
38
]
*Section II.4 assuming that

\mathcal{E}
has finite limits, enough projectives and is either cocomplete with a small set of generators (thus
permitting the small object argument) or such that every object in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is fibrant.
Quillen’s weak equivalences and fibrations include the
pointwise
weak equivalences and fibrations
defined here (as the former are defined using evaluation with respect to projective objects only) and the identity functor is a left Quillen functor
from Quillen’s model structure to the effective one. If effective epimorphisms split, then the two model structures coincide.

A class of model structures on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is also defined in
[
22
]
*Chapter II.
The construction is parametrised by a functor
G\colon\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}\to\mathsf{s}\mathcal{\mathsf{Set}}
with a left adjoint from which weak equivalences and fibrations are created.
If
\mathcal{E}
is complete and cocomplete and maps with the left lifting property with respect to fibrations are weak equivalences, one obtains a model structure.
This is quite different from the effective model structure and more in the spirit of generalizing
[
38
]
*Section II.4.

Finally, a model structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
has been obtained also in
[
27
]
, which appeared shortly after the first version of the present paper and was developed independently. Theorem 6.1 therein is a special case of our
Theorem

9.9
, obtained under the additional assumption that every object
of
\mathcal{E}
is a coproduct of
\mathbb{N}
-small objects (see
[
27
]
for details).

10
Descent and right properness

Having established the existence of the effective model structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
, we now
study some of its properties and those of its associated
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
.
There are many (essentially equivalent) ways of associating an
\infty
-category to a model category,
and our result will make little use of a concrete details of how it is done beyond some very general results.
For the sake of completeness, when we say
\infty
-category we mean quasicategory, and for a general category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a class of weak equivalences,
we define
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathcal{C})
as the
\infty
-category obtained by universally inverting the weak equivalences in
\mathcal{C}
.
We refer to
[
10
]
, especially its Chapter 7, for the general theory of such localisations.

We begin by studying the behaviour of colimits, using the notion of descent,
which was introduced in model categories by Rezk
[
42
]
as a part of development of higher topos theory. We show that
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
and hence
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
satisfies
descent
whenever
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is countably extensive.
This means that colimits in
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
satisfy the higher categorical version of the van Kampen property. In the case of pushouts, this is spelled out in
Proposition

10.1
below. As in the ordinary categorical case, a colimit in an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
satisfies descent if and only if it is preserved by the functor from
\mathcal{C}^{op}
to the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-category classified by the slice cartesian fibration. This is essentially proved in section 6.1.3 of
[
35
]
, see for example 6.1.3.9.

10.1
Model structure descent for pushouts.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a countably extensive category and let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{10}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{11}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{10}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{11}}

(10.1)

be a cube in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
.
Assume that the bottom face is a homotopy pushout and that the left and back faces are homotopy pullbacks.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The top face is a homotopy pushout,

(ii)

the right and front faces are homotopy pullbacks.

Proof.

Let us view
[1]
as a Reedy category consisting only of face operators.
We consider the Reedy model structure
[D^{\mathord{\mathrm{op}}},\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}]
of
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
over the Reedy category
D=[1]\times([1]\times[1])^{\mathord{\mathrm{op}}}
.
The significance of taking opposites on the latter two factors is that the Reedy category structure is inverted; the face operators become degeneracy operators.
Recall from the beginning of
Section

9
that we regard only certain (co)limits to be part of a model structure; the theory of Reedy model structures makes sense in this setting as seen in
Section

4
for the case of the Reedy weak factorisation system over
\Delta
.

The given cube (
10.1
) forms an object of this category by sending
(0,a,b)
to
Y_{ab}
and
(1,a,b)
to
X_{ab}
.
Recall that weak equivalences in the Reedy model structure are levelwise and homotopy pushouts and pullbacks are invariant under levelwise weak equivalences.
We replace the given cube by a cofibrant and fibrant object.
This reduces the claim to the case of (
10.1
) where all object are cofibrant and fibrant, all horizontal maps are cofibrations, and all vertical maps are fibrations.

Let us check the direction from
(i)
to
(ii)
, i.e., universality.
Take the pullback of the bottom face along
X_{11}\twoheadrightarrow Y_{11}
.
Since all vertical faces in (
10.1
) are homotopy pullbacks, we obtain a square weakly equivalent to the top face.
This reduces the claim to the situation where in addition all vertical faces in (
10.1
) are pullbacks.
Note that the cofibrancy assumptions are preserved by
(i)
of
Proposition

5.9
.

Denote
Q
the pushout in the bottom face.
Since
Y_{00}\to Y_{01}
is a levelwise complemented inclusion (
Proposition

3.17
),

P
is a van Kampen pushout by
Lemma

2.9
, in particular stable under pullback.
From universality, we obtain a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{11}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{11}}

(10.2)

where
P
is the pushout in the top face.
Since
X_{00}\to X_{01}
and
Y_{00}\to Y_{01}
are cofibrations, the bottom and top faces are homotopy pushouts exactly if the maps
P\to X_{11}
and
Q\to Y_{11}
are weak equivalences, respectively.
The goal thus follows from right properness applied to (
10.2
).

Let us check the direction from
(ii)
to
(i)
, i.e., effectivity.
Take the pushout in the horizontal faces.
Since all horizontal maps are cofibrations and the horizontal faces are homotopy pushouts, we obtain a cube weakly equivalent to the given cube.
This reduces the goal to the situation where all horizontal faces in (
10.1
) are pushouts, but note that we lose fibrancy properties involving
X_{11}
and
Y_{11}
.
The cube is now determined (up to isomorphism) by just the left and back faces.
Weakly equivalent left and back faces give rise to weakly equivalent cubes.

Since the back face is a homotopy pullback and the vertical maps are fibrations, the map
X_{00}\to Y_{00}\times_{Y_{01}}X_{01}
is a weak equivalence.
We apply the equivalence extension property of
Proposition

8.3
to this situation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{00}\times_{Y_{01}}X_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{01}}
i
\mathrel{\sim}
\mathrel{\sim}

We perform the same construction in the left face, obtaining
X_{10}^{\prime}
.
Now, the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{10}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{10}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{01}}

are weakly equivalent to the left and back faces, but are pullbacks.
We have thus reduced to the situation where additionally the left and back faces of (
10.1
) are pullbacks.

Having strictified the given homotopy pushouts and homotopy pullbacks, we proceed as follows.
The maps
X_{00}\to X_{01}
and
X_{00}\to X_{10}
are levelwise complemented inclusions by
Proposition

3.17
.
The bottom pushout is van Kampen by
(i)
of
Corollary

2.12
.
In particular, the right and front faces are pullbacks.
For them to be homotopy pullbacks, it suffices for
X_{11}\to Y_{11}
to be a fibration.
This holds by
(ii)
of
Lemma

3.18
.
∎

10.2
Model structure descent for coproducts.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
\alpha
-extensive category,
X\to Y
a morphism in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
and
S
an
\alpha
-small set.
Given a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

for each
s\in S
such that the induced morphism
\coprod_{s}Y_{s}\to Y
is a weak equivalence, the following are equivalent:

(i)

the square above is a homotopy pullback for each
s\in S
,

(ii)

the induced morphism
\coprod_{s}X_{s}\to X
is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

This follows from a simpler variant of the previous argument, for
\alpha
-small coproducts instead of pushouts.
This uses
(i)
instead of

(ii)
of
Lemma

3.18
.
∎

Propositions

10.1
and
10.2
have
an immediate counterpart at the
\infty
-categorical level.

10.3
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
\alpha
-extensive category.
The
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
has all
\alpha
-small colimits.
These colimits satisfy descent.

Proof.

It follows from
[
10
]
*Proposition 7.5.18 that
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
has finite limits and that finite homotopy limits in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
are sent to limits in
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
, the dual also holds for finite (homotopy) colimits. Moreover, one can deduce the same for
\alpha
-coproducts using
[
10
]
*Proposition 7.7.1 and Theorem 7.5.30.
This, together with
Propositions

10.1
and
10.2
immediately implies that pushouts and
\alpha
-coproducts satisfy descent in
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
.
From there,
[
35
]
*Proposition 4.4.2.6 shows that the existence of finite colimits and
\alpha
-coproducts implies the existence of all
\alpha
-small colimits in
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
. And given that a certain colimit satisfies descent if and only if it is preserved by the contravariant functor from
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
to the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories classified by the slice fibration,
[
35
]
*Proposition 4.4.2.7 shows that this implies that all
\alpha
-small colimits satisfy descent.
∎

We now move on to consider right properness of the effective model structure,
which will be the key to transfer local Cartesian closure from
\mathcal{E}
to
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
.

10.4
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a countably lextensive category. The effective model structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}

is right proper.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

7.6
using the argument
in
[
19
]
*Proposition 4.1, Second proof.
∎

10.5
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a countably lextensive category. If
\mathcal{E}
is locally Cartesian closed, then the

\infty
-category
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
is locally Cartesian closed.

Proof.

We first observe that if
\mathcal{E}
is countably lextensive and locally Cartesian closed, then
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is also locally Cartesian closed. Indeed, if
\mathcal{E}
is countably lextensive then
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
can be realised as the category of internal presheaves for the category object
\underline{\Delta}\in\mathcal{E}
. Such categories of internal presheaves over an internal category in a locally Cartesian closed categories are always locally Cartesian closed. Indeed, this follows from
[
31
]
*Theorem A4.2.1 and Proposition B2.3.16, using exactly the same argument as in the proof of
[
31
]
*Corollary B2.3.17 (which deals with the similar statement for toposes instead of locally Cartesian closed categories). Note that we are applying these results taking the category
\mathbb{D}
therein to be the canonical self-indexing of the base category
\mathcal{E}
, which satisfies the assumption of having
\mathcal{E}
-indexed products because of
[
31
]
*Lemma B1.4.7, part (iii) since
\mathcal{E}
is locally Cartesian closed.

An arbitrary map in
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
can always be represented by a fibration
p\colon X\to Y
between fibrant objects in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
with
X
cofibrant.
The functor
p^{*}
is a left adjoint functor since
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is locally Cartesian closed, it preserves cofibrations by
(i)
of
Proposition

5.9
and it preserves trivial cofibrations by the Frobenius property of
Proposition

7.6
.
It hence follows from
[
10
]
*Proposition 7.6.16 that the pullback functor
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})/Y\to\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})/X
admits a right adjoint (given by the action of the right adjoint of
p^{*}
on fibrant objects).
∎

We conclude this section by combining our results in the case
\mathcal{E}
is a Grothendieck topos.

10.6
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a Grothendieck topos. Then
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})

is locally Cartesian closed and has all small colimits, which satisfy descent. ∎

For a Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
, the effective model structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is typically not a model topos in the sense of Rezk
[
42
]
and
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})

is not a higher topos in the sense of Lurie
[
35
]
. Indeed, as we will see in
Example

11.8
, if
\mathcal{E}=\mathsf{Set}^{[1]}
, then the category of
0-truncated objects in
\operatorname{Ho}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
is neither a Grothendieck topos nor an elementary topos, as it does not have a subobject classifier. The situation
is reminiscent of that of Grothendieck toposes whose exact completion is neither a Grothendieck topos nor an elementary topos
[
37
]
.

11
A generalised Elmendorf theorem

Elmendorf’s theorem
[
15
,
50
]
states that the genuine equivariant model structure on
G
-spaces is equivalent to the projective model structure on presheaves of spaces on the category of orbits of
G
. In this section, we show as
Theorem

11.7
that, under the assumption that the category
\mathcal{E}
is completely lextensive and locally connected (in the sense of
Definition

11.1
below), then the
effective model category structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
models the
\infty
-category of small presheaves of spaces
on the full subcategory
\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}}
of connected objects in
\mathcal{E}
. Note that extension of Elmendorf’s theorem beyond the case of group action already appears in the literature
(cf.
[
8
,
14
,
12
]
). The work in
[
8
]
is especially close to what we prove in the present section.

Definition 11.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a lextensive category.

•

An object
X\in\mathcal{E}
is said to be
connected
if it is not the initial object and whenever
X=A\sqcup B
then
A={\mathord{\varnothing}}
or
B={\mathord{\varnothing}}
.

•

A lextensive category is said to be
locally connected
if every object is a van Kampen coproduct of connected objects.

The terminology of
Definition

11.1
is compatible with the notion of a locally connected Grothendieck topos. For example, the category of sheaves of set over a locally connected topological space is locally connected. The category of presheaves over a category
\mathcal{I}
is locally connected, its connected objects are called the “orbit” of
I
, i.e., the presheaves whose category of elements is connected, or equivalently whose colimits is a singleton. The coproduct completion of a category with finite limits is also a locally connected category.

Let us now fix a lextensive category
\mathcal{E}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}}
the full subcategory of of
\mathcal{E}
of connected objects. It is important to note that even if
\mathcal{E}
is a Grothendieck topos, this category is in general not a small category, as the next example illustrates.

Example 11.2
.

If
\mathcal{E}=\mathsf{Set}^{[1]}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Fam}}\mathsf{Set}
, then the connected objects of
\mathcal{E}
are the objects of the form
X\rightarrow*
for an arbitrary set
X
. In particular
\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}}
is equivalent to the category of all sets.
More generally, if
\mathcal{C}
is a category with finite limits, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Fam}}\mathcal{C}
is its coproduct completion, then
(\operatorname{\mathsf{Fam}}\mathcal{C})^{\mathsf{con}}=\mathcal{C}
.

11.3
.

Let
X
be a connected object in a lextensive category. Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{Set}}(X,\mathord{\relbar})
commutes with van Kampen coproducts.

Proof.

Given a map
f\colon X\rightarrow\coprod A_{i}
, then
X=\coprod X_{i}
where
X_{i}=X\operatorname*{\times}_{A}A_{i}
,
but as
X
is connected all the
X_{i}
except one are the initial object. As
X
is itself non-initial, then exactly one of the
X_{i}
is non initial
and hence
X=X_{i}
and the map
X\rightarrow\coprod A_{i}
factors into
X\rightarrow A_{i}
for a unique
i
.
∎

For a possibly large category
\mathcal{D}
, we write
\operatorname{\mathsf{Psh}}\mathcal{D}
for the category of small presheaves on
\mathcal{D}
, that is the category of presheaves on
\mathcal{D}
that can be written as small colimits of representables. We denote by
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}\mathcal{D}
the category of small simplicial presheaves, or equivalently simplicial objects in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Psh}}\mathcal{D}
. In general, limits of small presheaves can fail to be small, but if we assume that
\mathcal{D}
has
\alpha
-small limits, then
\operatorname{\mathsf{Psh}}\mathcal{D}
also has
\alpha
-small limits. This is proved in
[
11
]
as Theorem 4.3 applied to Example 4.1.1.

11.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a category with finite limits. Then
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}\mathcal{D}
carries the projective model structure, in which an arrow
f:X\rightarrow Y
if a fibration, trivial fibration or weak equivalence if and only if for all
d\in\mathcal{D}
, the arrow
f_{d}\colon X(d)\to Y(d)
is one.

Proof.

This is proved in
[
7
]
under the assumption that
\mathcal{D}
has all limits. However, the proof applies unchanged if we only assume that
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}\mathcal{D}
has finite limits, as long as we do not require that a model category has all limits, but only finite limits. Indeed the only use of limits in
\mathcal{D}
in the proof is to show that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Psh}}\mathcal{D}
has all limits. Moreover,
[
11
]
*Theorem 4.3 applied to Example 4.1.1 shows that if the category
\mathcal{D}
has finite limits then the category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Psh}}\mathcal{D}
of small presheaves on
\mathcal{D}
also has finite limits. Note that the existence of the corresponding weak factorisation system in
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}\mathcal{D}
follows from the generalised small object argument with respect to locally small class of arrows exactly as explained
in
[
7
]

∎

The claim of
Proposition

11.4
follows also from the assumption that
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}\mathcal{D}
has finite limits, which is a weaker condition than the existence of finite limits in
\mathcal{D}
.

11.5
.

The
\infty
-category associated to the projective model structure on
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}\mathcal{D}
is really the
\infty
-category of small presheaves of spaces on
\mathcal{D}
, essentially by the same argument as for small categories.

11.6
.

Given a locally connected countably lextensive category
\mathcal{E}
.

(i)

The restricted Yoneda embedding
\mathrm{y}\colon\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Psh}}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}})
is well-defined, fully faithful and preserves limits and all van Kampen coproducts.

(ii)

The restricted Yoneda embedding
\mathrm{y}\colon\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}})
is well-defined, fully faithful and preserves limits, pushouts along a cofibration, tensoring by objects of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathsf{sSet}
and colimits of sequences of cofibrations.

Proof.

For any connected object
X\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}}
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{Set}}(X,\mathord{\relbar})
preserves coproducts by
Lemma

11.3
, hence as every object
Y\in E
is a small van Kampen coproduct of connected objects, its image under the restricted Yoneda embedding is a small coproduct of representables, and hence is a small presheaf. This proves the existence and the preservation of coproducts by the Yoneda embedding. Preservation of limits is immediate.
It is fully faithful on connected objects by the Yoneda lemma, and this implies that it is fully faithful in general as morphisms between van Kampen coproducts of connected objects can be explicitly described as maps between their components.

The simplicial version is just the ordinary version applied levelwise in the simplicial direction so all results of part (ii) follow immediately. For the preservation of colimits we use the fact that a functor that preserves countable coproducts preserves pushouts of complemented inclusions and colimits of sequences of complemented inclusions, and all the colimits considered in the lemma are levelwise of this form.
∎

11.7
Generalised Elmendorf’s theorem.

Let
\mathcal{E}
a locally connected countably lextensive category.

(i)

A map in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is a cofibration, fibration or weak equivalence if and only if its image by the restricted Yoneda embedding is one for the projective model structure.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{E}
is in addition completely lextensive, then the restricted
Yoneda embedding induces an equivalence between the full subcategories of cofibrant objects of
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}})
. In particular it induces an equivalence of the corresponding
\infty
-categories.

Proof.

The (cofibration, trivial fibration) and (trivial cofibration, fibration) weak factorisation systems on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
are cofibrantly generated
in the (non-enriched) sense of
[
7
]
by the classes of arrows
\left\{i\mathbin{\cdot}E\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}i\in I_{\mathsf{sSet}},E\in\mathcal{E}\right\}
and

\left\{j\mathbin{\cdot}E\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}j\in I_{\mathsf{sSet}},E\in\mathcal{E}\right\}
.
As every object in
\mathcal{E}
is assumed to be a (van Kampen) coproduct of connected objects, one can restrict to
E\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}}
.
Because of
Lemma

11.6
, these generators are sent exactly to the generators of the projective model structure of
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}})
.

It immediately follows that an arrow in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
is a (trivial) fibration if and only if it is one in
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}})
as these classes are characterised by the same lifting property.

Moreover, also because of
Lemma

11.6
the restricted Yoneda embedding preserves coproducts and pushouts of the generating cofibrations,
transfinite composition of cofibrations and retracts.
Thus because of how (trivial) cofibrations are constructed in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
from the small object argument, it follows that their images in
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}})

are projective (trivial) cofibrations. Conversely, an arrow in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
which is a (trivial) cofibration in the projective model structure on
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}})

has the lifting property against all (trivial) fibrations in
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}})
, but as the restricted Yoneda embedding is fully faithful and
preserves (trivial) fibrations, it follows that it also has the lifting property against all (trivial) fibrations in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
and hence is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
.
This proves
(i)
for (trivial) cofibrations and (trivial) fibrations,
the case of equivalences also follows as an arrow is an equivalence if and only if it can be factored as trivial cofibration followed by a trivial fibration.

For
(ii)
, we just make one additional observation. If
\mathcal{E}
is completely lextensive, then any cofibrant object in
\operatorname{\mathsf{sPsh}}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}})
is in the image of the Yoneda embedding. Indeed, the image of
\mathrm{y}
contains the initial object and the generating cofibrations, and is closed under pushout of cofibrations, transfinite composition of cofibrations and retract (because it is closed under finite limits). Therefore, it contains all cofibrant objects. So as
\mathrm{y}
is fully faithful it is an equivalence of categories between the categories of cofibrant objects.
∎

In short,
Theorem

11.7
says that if
\mathcal{E}
is completely lextensive and locally connected, the effective model category structure on
\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}
of
Theorem

9.9
models the category of small presheaves of spaces on the large category
\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{con}}
. Note that we cannot quite say that the restricted Yoneda embedding is a Quillen equivalence because it does not admit an adjoint in general. However it follows from the theorem that if
\mathcal{E}
has all colimits, then it is a right Quillen equivalence. Note that a very general Elmendorf’s theorem was also proved in
[
8
]
*Theorem 3.1, which is similar to our version in many aspects. In fact, if we assume that
\mathcal{E}
is both complete and cocomplete then we can deduce our result from Chorny’s theorem.

Example 11.8
.

We take
\mathcal{E}
to be the category
\mathsf{Set}^{[1]}
of arrows in
\mathsf{Set}
.
It is completely lextensive and locally connected, and its connected objects are the ones of the form
X\to Y
where
Y
is the singleton.
Thus the category of connected objects can be identified with the category of sets, it hence follows by
Theorem

11.7
that
the category
\operatorname{Ho_{\infty}}(\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E})
can be identified with the category of small presheaves of spaces on the category of all sets. This
\infty
-category satisfies descent (all its colimits are van Kampen) and is locally cartesian closed, for example by
Theorem

10.5
and
Theorem

10.3
. But, it is not a (locally) presentable
\infty
-category, so is not an
\infty
-topos in the sense of
[
35
]
*Chapter 6. It is also not an elementary
\infty
-topos in the sense of
[
46
]
or
[
40
]
, for example its full subcategory of set-truncated objects is the category of small presheaves of sets on the category of all sets and is not an elementary topos as it does not have a subobject classifier. This category of set-truncated objects is however a pretopos (in the infinitary sense of the term) and is locally cartesian closed.

caries a non-Cartesian closed symmetric monoidal structure whose tensor is called the

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.91666pt}{-2.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

{

{

The homotopy theory of semisimplicial sets is well established.
Weak homotopy equivalences are defined as semisimplicial maps that become simplicial weak homotopy equivalences upon applying the functor
L
.

) is a fibration category.

Proof sketch.

The claim can be deduced from the existence of the fibration category of fibrant simplicial sets in
[
19
]
*Theorem 2.2.2.
The proof is analogous to the proof of
[
19
]
*Theorem 2.2.2 itself and depends on the following fact.
If
f\colon X\to Z
is a map between simplicial sets and
Uf
factors (in semisimplicial sets) as a composite of a cofibration
i\colon UX\to B

and a fibration
p\colon B\to UZ
, then
f
factors as a composite of
i^{\prime}\colon X\to Y
and
p^{\prime}\colon Y\to Z
such that
i=Ui^{\prime}
and
p=Up^{\prime}
.
(Note that, in particular,
B=UY
,
i
is a cofibration and
p
is a fibration.)
This holds by
[
49
]
*Theorem 2.1 and Addendum 2.2.
It will also rely the fact that
U
preserves and reflects weak equivalences by
[
24
]
*Lemma 2.2.1.

Compared to the proof of
[
19
]
*Theorem 2.2.2, the present argument requires only two modifications.
First, to construct a path object on a fibrant semisimplicial set
K
, we first apply the fact above (with
X={\mathord{\varnothing}}
,
Y=K
and
Z=1
) to obtain
a simplicial Kan complex
A
such that
UA=K
.
Then we obtain a path object on
K
by applying
U
to a path object on
A
.
Second, we observe that the facts above imply that a fibration in
{\text{\hbox to7.39pt{\vbox to4.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.22221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.91666pt}{-2.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.5}{0.0}{0.0}{0.5}{1.68056pt}{-4.47221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathsf{Set}}
is acyclic if and only if it is trivial (by reducing it to the same statement in
\mathsf{sSet}
).
Thus acyclic fibrations are stable under pullback.
6
6

6

This is non-constructive, because of the use of
[
49
]
. An alternative argument which works constructively can be found in
[
24
]
*Theorem 5.5.6. It shows that semisimplicial set have a weak model structure analogous to the Kan–Quillen model structure. Given that even constructively all semisimplicial sets are cofibrant this is enough to obtain that the full subcategory of fibrant objects is a fibration category.

∎

.

Pointwise weak equivalences, fibrations and trivial fibrations equip the category of Kan complexes in

with the structure of a fibration category.

Proof.

The proof is entirely analogous to the proof of
Theorem

1.7
except for the construction of path objects.
A path object on
X\in{\text{\hbox to7.39pt{\vbox to4.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.22221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.91666pt}{-2.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.5}{0.0}{0.0}{0.5}{1.68056pt}{-4.47221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{E}}
can be constructed as
X\to\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[1]\mathop{\pitchfork}X\twoheadrightarrow X\times X
as before.
However, there is no semisimplicial map
\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[1]\to\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[0]

(i.e.,
\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[0]
does not admit a cylinder object) and so the morphism
X\to\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[1]\mathop{\pitchfork}X

cannot be induced by functoriality of cotensors.
The problem can be fixed by constructing a “weak cylinder object” on
\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[0]
in the sense of
[
23
]
.

There is a unique map
\mathord{\Lambda}_{+}^{2}[2]\to\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[1]
.
It sends both
1
-simplices to the unique
1
-simplex of
\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[1]
.
We define
D
to be the pushout of this map along the trivial cofibration
\mathord{\Lambda}_{+}^{2}[2]\to\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[2]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Lambda}_{+}^{2}[2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\text{.}}

Thus
D
has two
0
-simplices
b
and
x
, two
1
-simplices
f\colon b\to x
and
e\colon b\to b
and
a unique
2
-simplex that witnesses that
f\circ e\sim e
.
Informally speaking, this forces
e
to behave as an “identity cell” of
b
.
More precisely, we obtain a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\partial\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[0]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
b
e
\mathrel{\sim}

which upon cotensoring into
X\in{\text{\hbox to7.39pt{\vbox to4.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.22221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.91666pt}{-2.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.5}{0.0}{0.0}{0.5}{1.68056pt}{-4.47221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

yields

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\Delta}_{+}[1]\mathop{\pitchfork}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathop{\pitchfork}X\text{.}}
\mathrel{\sim}

When

is a Kan complex, the right vertical morphism is a trivial fibration and hence it has a section
by

.
We obtain the required factorisation by composing

to it gives, up to isomorphism, the map

, seen as a functor

, its right Kan extension along

exists and is a pointwise right Kan extension. Indeed, the pointwise right Kan extension computed at

should be

RV=\lim_{[m]\to[n]\in E}V([m])

where

. This category is countable, so as

\mathsf{s}\mathcal{E}\to{\text{\hbox to7.39pt{\vbox to4.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.22221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

.

Proof.

The theorem is valid for simplicial and semisimplicial sets, i.e., in the case of
\mathcal{E}=\mathsf{Set}
.
As both
U
and
R
commute with evaluation at
E\in\mathcal{E}
and weak equivalences and fibrations are
detected by these evaluations, it follows that:

•

U
and
R
preserve fibrant objects and are morphisms of fibrations categories;

•

the unit and counit of the adjunctions are weak equivalences on fibrant objects. ∎

We now move to the case of a countably lextensive category
\mathcal{E}
.
Despite the fact that the theorem concerns only the fibrant objects of

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

.

This is analogous to the characterisation of weak equivalences between simplicial objects in the model structure of

The weak equivalences, fibrations and cofibrations defined in this section do not form a model structure on

,
but we can still prove that they are sufficiently well-behaved for our purposes.
For example, the definition of weak equivalences immediately implies that trivial cofibrations are weak equivalences.
On the other hand, not all trivial fibrations are weak equivalences.

.

\mathcal{E}
is countably lextensive then

*Proposition 2.3.3 and properties of the classes established in this section. In fact, as every object of

is cofibrant, this is even a right semi-model category, as long as we use the definition of a semi-model category in
[

]
and not that in
[

]
(see
